People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Well… I guess what brings me in is I just don’t have the push I used to. I used to go to choir, and I had my quilting things, and I’d visit folks… you know, like when somebody expects you and you say you’ll come by. My neighbor used to make these little lemon cookies—well, anyway.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, sometimes I think about things I should’ve done, like calling somebody back, or keeping the kitchen nicer. My mother used to say dishes don’t wash themselves, and she was right about that.

I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have no interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s part of it. I used to like choir, and quilting too—my hands knew what to do with the squares, you know, lining them up just right. And visiting folks after church or when somebody expected me.\n\nNow I just don’t have the push for it. I’ll think, “I ought to go,” and then I’m still sitting there at home with the TV on low, not really watching it. People are probably waiting on me, but I just… don’t get myself moving.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

Time gets funny when you’re not getting up to go in every morning—Mondays feel like Tuesdays, and then the mail comes late and I think the whole day’s off.\n\nBut yes, it’s been building since then, little by little.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever thought about ending your life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 67 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I get low and I sit too much, and sometimes I wonder what the point of a day is when nothing much happens, but ending my life… no, I haven’t really thought about doing that.

I did have an aunt who used to say you don’t know what burdens people carry, and that always stuck with me. She kept all her old buttons in a coffee tin—funny what you remember. But no, doctor, I don’t believe I’ve had thoughts like that.

Time gets funny when you’re not getting up to go in every morning—Mondays feel like Tuesdays, and then the mail comes late and I think the whole day’s off.\n\nBut yes, it’s been building since then, little by little.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I get low and I sit too much, and sometimes I wonder what the point of a day is when nothing much happens, but ending my life… no, I haven’t really thought about doing that.\n\nI did have an aunt who used to say you don’t know what burdens people carry, and that always stuck with me. She kept all her old buttons in a coffee tin—funny what you remember. But no, doctor, I don’t believe I’ve had thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your lack of interest in activities and flat mood are interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does. I don’t keep up with things like I used to. I had to step back from leading choir, and I used to help with those little projects at the senior center—you know, cutting paper, sorting donations, whatever they needed. But then I’d say I would do something and it just wouldn’t get done.\n\nAnd people notice. The church ladies check on me more now, calling or stopping by, like they’re making sure I haven’t forgotten the world. It’s kind of them, but it makes me feel… well, like I’m not managing the way I ought to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. My mother used to say dishes don’t wash themselves, and she was right about that.\n\nBut guilty all the time? I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have no interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s part of it. I used to like choir, and quilting too—my hands knew what to do with the squares, you know, lining them up just right. And visiting folks after church or when somebody expected me.\n\nNow I just don’t have the push for it. I’ll think, “I ought to go,” and then I’m still sitting there at home with the TV on low, not really watching it. People are probably waiting on me, but I just… don’t get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been… well, since I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. Time gets funny when you’re not getting up to go in every morning—Mondays feel like Tuesdays, and then the mail comes late and I think the whole day’s off.\n\nBut yes, it’s been building since then, little by little.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I get low and I sit too much, and sometimes I wonder what the point of a day is when nothing much happens, but ending my life… no, I haven’t really thought about doing that.\n\nI did have an aunt who used to say you don’t know what burdens people carry, and that always stuck with me. She kept all her old buttons in a coffee tin—funny what you remember. But no, doctor, I don’t believe I’ve had thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your lack of interest in activities and flat mood are interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does. I don’t keep up with things like I used to. I had to step back from leading choir, and I used to help with those little projects at the senior center—you know, cutting paper, sorting donations, whatever they needed. But then I’d say I would do something and it just wouldn’t get done.\n\nAnd people notice. The church ladies check on me more now, calling or stopping by, like they’re making sure I haven’t forgotten the world. It’s kind of them, but it makes me feel… well, like I’m not managing the way I ought to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your current state is worse than it was at the beginning?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is worse than when it started. At first, after I retired from the school office—oh, a little over two years ago now—it felt more like just adjusting, you know? Losing the routine, not hearing the copier jam or the children in the hall, all that noise you don’t think you’ll miss.\n\nBut it’s been building since then. Not all at once. More like when dust gathers on a shelf and you don’t notice until the sun hits it. So yes, I’d say it’s worse now than at the beginning.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Hopeless… I don’t know if I’d use that word. Some mornings I don’t think much past breakfast, if I’m honest. I’ll put the kettle on and then remember my neighbor used to say tea tastes different depending on the cup, which sounds silly, but she had these blue cups with little chips in them.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I just don’t have the push I used to. I used to go to choir, and I had my quilting things, and I’d visit folks… you know, like when somebody expects you and you say you’ll come by. My neighbor used to make these little lemon cookies—well, anyway.\n\nMost days now I just sit at home with the TV on low, not really watching it. I know people are probably waiting on me or wondering where I am, but I just can’t seem to get myself up and go.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to do, like choir and quilting?\n\nPatient: Not like I used to, no. I mean, I still know choir was nice, and quilting too… I’ve got pieces folded up in a basket by the chair, the blue one with the little flowers, I think. But I just don’t feel the push to pick it up or go out.\n\nMost days I just end up sitting there with the TV turned down low, and then the day’s gone. I think, “Oh, I should’ve gone by, they’re probably expecting me,” but I just… don’t get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in the things you do?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can remember liking choir—there was one hymn, I can’t think of the name, but Mrs. Allen used to sing it too loud, bless her heart—and quilting used to feel good in my hands.\n\nBut now it’s more like… I know those things are there, and I know folks might be expecting me to come by, but I just sit at home with the TV low. I don’t get much pleasure out of it, not like before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. It’s more… flat, I guess. Like the house is quiet, and I sit there, and then I notice the dust on the TV stand—my sister used to say you could write your name in it if you didn’t keep up with it.\n\nBut sad… I’m not sure. I don’t sit and cry much or anything. It’s just hard to say what it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I don’t really weigh myself much. I haven’t noticed anything big like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I just don’t have the push I used to. I used to go to choir, and I had my quilting things, and I’d visit folks… you know, like when somebody expects you and you say you’ll come by. My neighbor used to make these little lemon cookies—well, anyway.\n\nMost days now I just sit at home with the TV on low, not really watching it. I know people are probably waiting on me or wondering where I am, but I just can’t seem to get myself up and go.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to do, like choir and quilting?\n\nPatient: Not like I used to, no. I mean, I still know choir was nice, and quilting too… I’ve got pieces folded up in a basket by the chair, the blue one with the little flowers, I think. But I just don’t feel the push to pick it up or go out.\n\nMost days I just end up sitting there with the TV turned down low, and then the day’s gone. I think, “Oh, I should’ve gone by, they’re probably expecting me,” but I just… don’t get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in the things you do?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can remember liking choir—there was one hymn, I can’t think of the name, but Mrs. Allen used to sing it too loud, bless her heart—and quilting used to feel good in my hands.\n\nBut now it’s more like… I know those things are there, and I know folks might be expecting me to come by, but I just sit at home with the TV low. I don’t get much pleasure out of it, not like before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. It’s more… flat, I guess. Like the house is quiet, and I sit there, and then I notice the dust on the TV stand—my sister used to say you could write your name in it if you didn’t keep up with it.\n\nBut sad… I’m not sure. I don’t sit and cry much or anything. It’s just hard to say what it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I don’t really weigh myself much. The scale’s in the bathroom but it’s tucked behind some towels, and I keep meaning to move it because it gets dusty there.\n\nMy clothes seem about the same, I guess. I haven’t noticed anything big like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, sometimes I think about things I should’ve done, like calling somebody back, or keeping the kitchen nicer. My mother used to say dishes don’t wash themselves, and she was right about that.\n\nBut guilty all the time? I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have no interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s part of it. I used to like choir, and quilting too—my hands knew what to do with the squares, you know, lining them up just right. And visiting folks after church or when somebody expected me.\n\nNow I just don’t have the push for it. I’ll think, “I ought to go,” and then I’m still sitting there at home with the TV on low, not really watching it. People are probably waiting on me, but I just… don’t get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been… well, since I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. Time gets funny when you’re not getting up to go in every morning—Mondays feel like Tuesdays, and then the mail comes late and I think the whole day’s off.\n\nBut yes, it’s been building since then, little by little.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I get low and I sit too much, and sometimes I wonder what the point of a day is when nothing much happens, but ending my life… no, I haven’t really thought about doing that.\n\nI did have an aunt who used to say you don’t know what burdens people carry, and that always stuck with me. She kept all her old buttons in a coffee tin—funny what you remember. But no, doctor, I don’t believe I’ve had thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your lack of interest in activities and flat mood are interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does. I don’t keep up with things like I used to. I had to step back from leading choir, and I used to help with those little projects at the senior center—you know, cutting paper, sorting donations, whatever they needed. But then I’d say I would do something and it just wouldn’t get done.\n\nAnd people notice. The church ladies check on me more now, calling or stopping by, like they’re making sure I haven’t forgotten the world. It’s kind of them, but it makes me feel… well, like I’m not managing the way I ought to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your current state is worse than it was at the beginning?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is worse than when it started. At first, after I retired from the school office—oh, a little over two years ago now—it felt more like just adjusting, you know? Losing the routine, not hearing the copier jam or the children in the hall, all that noise you don’t think you’ll miss.\n\nBut it’s been building since then. Not all at once. More like when dust gathers on a shelf and you don’t notice until the sun hits it. So yes, I’d say it’s worse now than at the beginning.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless about your future?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know if I’d use that word. Some mornings I don’t think much past breakfast, if I’m honest. I’ll put the kettle on and then remember my neighbor used to say tea tastes different depending on the cup, which sounds silly, but she had these blue cups with little chips in them.\n\nBut about the future… I’m not sure. I don’t sit there thinking it’s all ruined or anything. It just feels kind of blank sometimes, like I can’t quite picture it. Is that what you mean by hopeless?\n\nDoctor: How many months would you say you've been feeling this flat and unmotivated?\n\nPatient: I guess… a little over two years, if I’m counting right. It started building after I retired from the school office. At first I thought it was just because I didn’t have my desk anymore, or the morning bell, and you know I kept all the paper clips in a little tin—funny the things you remember.\n\nBut yes… months-wise, more than twenty-four months. A little over two years.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yes… I think it does, in some ways. I mean, I still try to manage, but I’ve had to step back from things I used to do. I was leading choir for a while—well, helping lead, really, because Mrs. Donnelly always knew the alto parts better than me—and I used to help with projects at the senior center too.

I don’t get much pleasure out of it, not like before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. It’s more… flat, I guess. I don’t sit and cry much or anything. It’s just hard to say what it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I don’t really weigh myself much. I haven’t noticed anything big like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I just don’t have the push I used to. I used to go to choir, and I had my quilting things, and I’d visit folks… you know, like when somebody expects you and you say you’ll come by. My neighbor used to make these little lemon cookies—well, anyway.\n\nMost days now I just sit at home with the TV on low, not really watching it. I know people are probably waiting on me or wondering where I am, but I just can’t seem to get myself up and go.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to do, like choir and quilting?\n\nPatient: Not like I used to, no. I mean, I still know choir was nice, and quilting too… I’ve got pieces folded up in a basket by the chair, the blue one with the little flowers, I think. But I just don’t feel the push to pick it up or go out.\n\nMost days I just end up sitting there with the TV turned down low, and then the day’s gone. I think, “Oh, I should’ve gone by, they’re probably expecting me,” but I just… don’t get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in the things you do?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can remember liking choir—there was one hymn, I can’t think of the name, but Mrs. Allen used to sing it too loud, bless her heart—and quilting used to feel good in my hands.\n\nBut now it’s more like… I know those things are there, and I know folks might be expecting me to come by, but I just sit at home with the TV low. I don’t get much pleasure out of it, not like before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. It’s more… flat, I guess. Like the house is quiet, and I sit there, and then I notice the dust on the TV stand—my sister used to say you could write your name in it if you didn’t keep up with it.\n\nBut sad… I’m not sure. I don’t sit and cry much or anything. It’s just hard to say what it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I don’t really weigh myself much. The scale’s in the bathroom but it’s tucked behind some towels, and I keep meaning to move it because it gets dusty there.\n\nMy clothes seem about the same, I guess. I haven’t noticed anything big like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, sometimes I think about things I should’ve done, like calling somebody back, or keeping the kitchen nicer. My mother used to say dishes don’t wash themselves, and she was right about that.\n\nBut guilty all the time? I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have no interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s part of it. I used to like choir, and quilting too—my hands knew what to do with the squares, you know, lining them up just right. And visiting folks after church or when somebody expected me.\n\nNow I just don’t have the push for it. I’ll think, “I ought to go,” and then I’m still sitting there at home with the TV on low, not really watching it. People are probably waiting on me, but I just… don’t get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been… well, since I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. Time gets funny when you’re not getting up to go in every morning—Mondays feel like Tuesdays, and then the mail comes late and I think the whole day’s off.\n\nBut yes, it’s been building since then, little by little.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I get low and I sit too much, and sometimes I wonder what the point of a day is when nothing much happens, but ending my life… no, I haven’t really thought about doing that.\n\nI did have an aunt who used to say you don’t know what burdens people carry, and that always stuck with me. She kept all her old buttons in a coffee tin—funny what you remember. But no, doctor, I don’t believe I’ve had thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your lack of interest in activities and flat mood are interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does. I don’t keep up with things like I used to. I had to step back from leading choir, and I used to help with those little projects at the senior center—you know, cutting paper, sorting donations, whatever they needed. But then I’d say I would do something and it just wouldn’t get done.\n\nAnd people notice. The church ladies check on me more now, calling or stopping by, like they’re making sure I haven’t forgotten the world. It’s kind of them, but it makes me feel… well, like I’m not managing the way I ought to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your current state is worse than it was at the beginning?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is worse than when it started. At first, after I retired from the school office—oh, a little over two years ago now—it felt more like just adjusting, you know? Losing the routine, not hearing the copier jam or the children in the hall, all that noise you don’t think you’ll miss.\n\nBut it’s been building since then. Not all at once. More like when dust gathers on a shelf and you don’t notice until the sun hits it. So yes, I’d say it’s worse now than at the beginning.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless about your future?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know if I’d use that word. Some mornings I don’t think much past breakfast, if I’m honest. I’ll put the kettle on and then remember my neighbor used to say tea tastes different depending on the cup, which sounds silly, but she had these blue cups with little chips in them.\n\nBut about the future… I’m not sure. I don’t sit there thinking it’s all ruined or anything. It just feels kind of blank sometimes, like I can’t quite picture it. Is that what you mean by hopeless?\n\nDoctor: How many months would you say you've been feeling this flat and unmotivated?\n\nPatient: I guess… a little over two years, if I’m counting right. It started building after I retired from the school office. At first I thought it was just because I didn’t have my desk anymore, or the morning bell, and you know I kept all the paper clips in a little tin—funny the things you remember.\n\nBut yes… months-wise, more than twenty-four months. A little over two years.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your current state is significantly impacting your ability to take care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, in some ways. I mean, I still try to manage, but I’ve had to step back from things I used to do. I promise I’ll bring papers or call someone or organize something, and then it just… doesn’t happen right. So church friends check on me more now than they used to. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Severely… I don’t know if I’d say severely, like I can’t do anything at all. I still get through some things.

But it does get in the way. I had to step back from helping lead choir, and from the senior-center projects too, because I’d say I’d do something—bring papers, call someone, sort things out—and then I just wouldn’t keep up with it. And you know, choir needs you to be there, especially when the altos get lost—though Mrs. Donnelly always had a good ear for that.

So… yes, it affects my daily life, but maybe not the very simplest things every single day. My church friends check on me more now than they used to. That part makes me feel a bit ashamed.

I know people are probably waiting on me or wondering where I am, but I just can’t seem to get myself up and go.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to do, like choir and quilting?\n\nPatient: Not like I used to, no. I mean, I still know choir was nice, and quilting too… I’ve got pieces folded up in a basket by the chair, the blue one with the little flowers, I think. But I just don’t feel the push to pick it up or go out.\n\nMost days I just end up sitting there with the TV turned down low, and then the day’s gone. I think, “Oh, I should’ve gone by, they’re probably expecting me,” but I just… don’t get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in the things you do?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can remember liking choir—there was one hymn, I can’t think of the name, but Mrs. Allen used to sing it too loud, bless her heart—and quilting used to feel good in my hands.\n\nBut now it’s more like… I know those things are there, and I know folks might be expecting me to come by, but I just sit at home with the TV low. I don’t get much pleasure out of it, not like before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. It’s more… flat, I guess. Like the house is quiet, and I sit there, and then I notice the dust on the TV stand—my sister used to say you could write your name in it if you didn’t keep up with it.\n\nBut sad… I’m not sure. I don’t sit and cry much or anything. It’s just hard to say what it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I don’t really weigh myself much. The scale’s in the bathroom but it’s tucked behind some towels, and I keep meaning to move it because it gets dusty there.\n\nMy clothes seem about the same, I guess. I haven’t noticed anything big like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, sometimes I think about things I should’ve done, like calling somebody back, or keeping the kitchen nicer. My mother used to say dishes don’t wash themselves, and she was right about that.\n\nBut guilty all the time? I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have no interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s part of it. I used to like choir, and quilting too—my hands knew what to do with the squares, you know, lining them up just right. I’ll think, “I ought to go,” and then I’m still sitting there at home with the TV on low, not really watching it. People are probably waiting on me, but I just… don’t get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been… well, since I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. Time gets funny when you’re not getting up to go in every morning—Mondays feel like Tuesdays, and then the mail comes late and I think the whole day’s off.\n\nBut yes, it’s been building since then, little by little.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I get low and I sit too much, and sometimes I wonder what the point of a day is when nothing much happens, but ending my life… no, I haven’t really thought about doing that.\n\nI did have an aunt who used to say you don’t know what burdens people carry, and that always stuck with me. At first, after I retired from the school office—oh, a little over two years ago now—it felt more like just adjusting, you know? significantly impacting your ability to take care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, in some ways. I still get through some things.\n\nBut it does get in the way. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I just don’t have the push I used to. I used to go to choir, and I had my quilting things, and I’d visit folks… you know, like when somebody expects you and you say you’ll come by. My neighbor used to make these little lemon cookies—well, anyway.\n\nMost days now I just sit at home with the TV on low, not really watching it. I know people are probably waiting on me or wondering where I am, but I just can’t seem to get myself up and go.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to do, like choir and quilting?\n\nPatient: Not like I used to, no. I mean, I still know choir was nice, and quilting too… I’ve got pieces folded up in a basket by the chair, the blue one with the little flowers, I think. But I just don’t feel the push to pick it up or go out.\n\nMost days I just end up sitting there with the TV turned down low, and then the day’s gone. I think, “Oh, I should’ve gone by, they’re probably expecting me,” but I just… don’t get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in the things you do?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can remember liking choir—there was one hymn, I can’t think of the name, but Mrs. Allen used to sing it too loud, bless her heart—and quilting used to feel good in my hands.\n\nBut now it’s more like… I know those things are there, and I know folks might be expecting me to come by, but I just sit at home with the TV low. I don’t get much pleasure out of it, not like before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. It’s more… flat, I guess. Like the house is quiet, and I sit there, and then I notice the dust on the TV stand—my sister used to say you could write your name in it if you didn’t keep up with it.\n\nBut sad… I’m not sure. I don’t sit and cry much or anything. It’s just hard to say what it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I don’t really weigh myself much. The scale’s in the bathroom but it’s tucked behind some towels, and I keep meaning to move it because it gets dusty there.\n\nMy clothes seem about the same, I guess. I haven’t noticed anything big like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, sometimes I think about things I should’ve done, like calling somebody back, or keeping the kitchen nicer. My mother used to say dishes don’t wash themselves, and she was right about that.\n\nBut guilty all the time? I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have no interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s part of it. I used to like choir, and quilting too—my hands knew what to do with the squares, you know, lining them up just right. And visiting folks after church or when somebody expected me.\n\nNow I just don’t have the push for it. I’ll think, “I ought to go,” and then I’m still sitting there at home with the TV on low, not really watching it. People are probably waiting on me, but I just… don’t get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been… well, since I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. Time gets funny when you’re not getting up to go in every morning—Mondays feel like Tuesdays, and then the mail comes late and I think the whole day’s off.\n\nBut yes, it’s been building since then, little by little.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I get low and I sit too much, and sometimes I wonder what the point of a day is when nothing much happens, but ending my life… no, I haven’t really thought about doing that.\n\nI did have an aunt who used to say you don’t know what burdens people carry, and that always stuck with me. She kept all her old buttons in a coffee tin—funny what you remember. But no, doctor, I don’t believe I’ve had thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your lack of interest in activities and flat mood are interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does. I don’t keep up with things like I used to. I had to step back from leading choir, and I used to help with those little projects at the senior center—you know, cutting paper, sorting donations, whatever they needed. But then I’d say I would do something and it just wouldn’t get done.\n\nAnd people notice. The church ladies check on me more now, calling or stopping by, like they’re making sure I haven’t forgotten the world. It’s kind of them, but it makes me feel… well, like I’m not managing the way I ought to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your current state is worse than it was at the beginning?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is worse than when it started. At first, after I retired from the school office—oh, a little over two years ago now—it felt more like just adjusting, you know? Losing the routine, not hearing the copier jam or the children in the hall, all that noise you don’t think you’ll miss.\n\nBut it’s been building since then. Not all at once. More like when dust gathers on a shelf and you don’t notice until the sun hits it. So yes, I’d say it’s worse now than at the beginning.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless about your future?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know if I’d use that word. Some mornings I don’t think much past breakfast, if I’m honest. I’ll put the kettle on and then remember my neighbor used to say tea tastes different depending on the cup, which sounds silly, but she had these blue cups with little chips in them.\n\nBut about the future… I’m not sure. I don’t sit there thinking it’s all ruined or anything. It just feels kind of blank sometimes, like I can’t quite picture it. Is that what you mean by hopeless?\n\nDoctor: How many months would you say you've been feeling this flat and unmotivated?\n\nPatient: I guess… a little over two years, if I’m counting right. It started building after I retired from the school office. At first I thought it was just because I didn’t have my desk anymore, or the morning bell, and you know I kept all the paper clips in a little tin—funny the things you remember.\n\nBut yes… months-wise, more than twenty-four months. A little over two years.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your current state is significantly impacting your ability to take care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, in some ways. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it does. I don’t mean to push people away, but I just don’t have the push I used to. Choir, quilting, going to visit folks… I used to do those things without thinking much about it.

Now most days I’m just at home with the TV on low, and I know people are expecting me, or wondering where I am. The quilting group had those little squares they wanted finished—green ones, I think, though green never matches the way people think it will—and I just didn’t go.

So, yes… with friends, especially, it gets in the way. I don’t feel very much like joining in.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I just don’t have the push I used to. I used to go to choir, and I had my quilting things, and I’d visit folks… you know, like when somebody expects you and you say you’ll come by. My neighbor used to make these little lemon cookies—well, anyway.\n\nMost days now I just sit at home with the TV on low, not really watching it. I know people are probably waiting on me or wondering where I am, but I just can’t seem to get myself up and go.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to do, like choir and quilting?\n\nPatient: Not like I used to, no. I mean, I still know choir was nice, and quilting too… I’ve got pieces folded up in a basket by the chair, the blue one with the little flowers, I think. But I just don’t feel the push to pick it up or go out.\n\nMost days I just end up sitting there with the TV turned down low, and then the day’s gone. I think, “Oh, I should’ve gone by, they’re probably expecting me,” but I just… don’t get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in the things you do?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can remember liking choir—there was one hymn, I can’t think of the name, but Mrs. Allen used to sing it too loud, bless her heart—and quilting used to feel good in my hands.\n\nBut now it’s more like… I know those things are there, and I know folks might be expecting me to come by, but I just sit at home with the TV low. I don’t get much pleasure out of it, not like before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. It’s more… flat, I guess. Like the house is quiet, and I sit there, and then I notice the dust on the TV stand—my sister used to say you could write your name in it if you didn’t keep up with it.\n\nBut sad… I’m not sure. I don’t sit and cry much or anything. It’s just hard to say what it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I don’t really weigh myself much. The scale’s in the bathroom but it’s tucked behind some towels, and I keep meaning to move it because it gets dusty there.\n\nMy clothes seem about the same, I guess. I haven’t noticed anything big like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, sometimes I think about things I should’ve done, like calling somebody back, or keeping the kitchen nicer. My mother used to say dishes don’t wash themselves, and she was right about that.\n\nBut guilty all the time? I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have no interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s part of it. I used to like choir, and quilting too—my hands knew what to do with the squares, you know, lining them up just right. And visiting folks after church or when somebody expected me.\n\nNow I just don’t have the push for it. I’ll think, “I ought to go,” and then I’m still sitting there at home with the TV on low, not really watching it. People are probably waiting on me, but I just… don’t get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been… well, since I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. Time gets funny when you’re not getting up to go in every morning—Mondays feel like Tuesdays, and then the mail comes late and I think the whole day’s off.\n\nBut yes, it’s been building since then, little by little.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I get low and I sit too much, and sometimes I wonder what the point of a day is when nothing much happens, but ending my life… no, I haven’t really thought about doing that.\n\nI did have an aunt who used to say you don’t know what burdens people carry, and that always stuck with me. She kept all her old buttons in a coffee tin—funny what you remember. But no, doctor, I don’t believe I’ve had thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your lack of interest in activities and flat mood are interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does. I don’t keep up with things like I used to. I had to step back from leading choir, and I used to help with those little projects at the senior center—you know, cutting paper, sorting donations, whatever they needed. But then I’d say I would do something and it just wouldn’t get done.\n\nAnd people notice. The church ladies check on me more now, calling or stopping by, like they’re making sure I haven’t forgotten the world. It’s kind of them, but it makes me feel… well, like I’m not managing the way I ought to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your current state is worse than it was at the beginning?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is worse than when it started. At first, after I retired from the school office—oh, a little over two years ago now—it felt more like just adjusting, you know? Losing the routine, not hearing the copier jam or the children in the hall, all that noise you don’t think you’ll miss.\n\nBut it’s been building since then. Not all at once. More like when dust gathers on a shelf and you don’t notice until the sun hits it. So yes, I’d say it’s worse now than at the beginning.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless about your future?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know if I’d use that word. Some mornings I don’t think much past breakfast, if I’m honest. I’ll put the kettle on and then remember my neighbor used to say tea tastes different depending on the cup, which sounds silly, but she had these blue cups with little chips in them.\n\nBut about the future… I’m not sure. I don’t sit there thinking it’s all ruined or anything. It just feels kind of blank sometimes, like I can’t quite picture it. Is that what you mean by hopeless?\n\nDoctor: How many months would you say you've been feeling this flat and unmotivated?\n\nPatient: I guess… a little over two years, if I’m counting right. It started building after I retired from the school office. At first I thought it was just because I didn’t have my desk anymore, or the morning bell, and you know I kept all the paper clips in a little tin—funny the things you remember.\n\nBut yes… months-wise, more than twenty-four months. A little over two years.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your current state is significantly impacting your ability to take care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, in some ways. I mean, I still try to manage, but I’ve had to step back from things I used to do. I was leading choir for a while—well, helping lead, really, because Mrs. Donnelly always knew the alto parts better than me—and I used to help with projects at the senior center too.\n\nBut I don’t keep up with what I say I’ll do. I promise I’ll bring papers or call someone or organize something, and then it just… doesn’t happen right. So church friends check on me more now than they used to. They’ll call or stop by, which is kind of them, but it makes me feel a little embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite?\n\nPatient: My appetite… I’m not sure it’s changed in any big way. I eat when I remember to, mostly. Breakfast is usually toast, unless the bread’s gone stale, and then I think about going to the shop but the shop moved the biscuits to a different aisle last year and I still don’t like that.\n\nBut, no… I haven’t really noticed a significant change, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your current state is severely impacting your daily life, making it difficult to carry out even the simplest tasks?\n\nPatient: Severely… I don’t know if I’d say severely, like I can’t do anything at all. I still get through some things.\n\nBut it does get in the way. I was leading choir for a while—well, helping lead, really, because Mrs. Donnelly always knew the alto parts better than me—and I used to help with projects at the senior center too.\n\nBut I don’t keep up with what I say I’ll do. I promise I’ll bring papers or call someone or organize something, and then it just… doesn’t happen right. So church friends check on me more now than they used to. They’ll call or stop by, which is kind of them, but it makes me feel a little embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite?\n\nPatient: My appetite… I’m not sure it’s changed in any big way. I eat when I remember to, mostly. Breakfast is usually toast, unless the bread’s gone stale, and then I think about going to the shop but the shop moved the biscuits to a different aisle last year and I still don’t like that.\n\nBut, no… I haven’t really noticed a significant change, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your current state is severely impacting your daily life, making it difficult to carry out even the simplest tasks?\n\nPatient: Severely… I don’t know if I’d say severely, like I can’t do anything at all. I still get through some things.\n\nBut it does get in the way. I had to step back from helping lead choir, and from the senior-center projects too, because I’d say I’d do something—bring papers, call someone, sort things out—and then I just wouldn’t keep up with it. And you know, choir needs you to be there, especially when the altos get lost—though Mrs. Donnelly always had a good ear for that.\n\nSo… yes, it affects my daily life, but maybe not the very simplest things every single day. My church friends check on me more now than they used to. That part makes me feel a bit ashamed.\n\nDoctor: Has your current state lasted for more than two years?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s been building since I retired from the school office, and that was a little over two years ago now. I remember because they had that cake with the blue icing, and it got all over the napkins—funny the things you remember.\n\nBut yes… more than two years, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your lack of interest and flat mood are significantly interfering with your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does. I don’t mean to push people away, but I just don’t have the push I used to. Choir, quilting, going to visit folks… I used to do those things without thinking much about it.\n\nNow most days I’m just at home with the TV on low, and I know people are expecting me, or wondering where I am. The quilting group had those little squares they wanted finished—green ones, I think, though green never matches the way people think it will—and I just didn’t go.\n\nSo, yes… with friends, especially, it gets in the way. I don’t feel very much like joining in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I just don’t have the push I used to. I used to go to choir, and I had my quilting things, and I’d visit folks… you know, like when somebody expects you and you say you’ll come by. My neighbor used to make these little lemon cookies—well, anyway.\n\nMost days now I just sit at home with the TV on low, not really watching it. I know people are probably waiting on me or wondering where I am, but I just can’t seem to get myself up and go.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to do, like choir and quilting?\n\nPatient: Not like I used to, no. I mean, I still know choir was nice, and quilting too… I’ve got pieces folded up in a basket by the chair, the blue one with the little flowers, I think. But I just don’t feel the push to pick it up or go out.\n\nMost days I just end up sitting there with the TV turned down low, and then the day’s gone. I think, “Oh, I should’ve gone by, they’re probably expecting me,” but I just… don’t get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in the things you do?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can remember liking choir—there was one hymn, I can’t think of the name, but Mrs. Allen used to sing it too loud, bless her heart—and quilting used to feel good in my hands.\n\nBut now it’s more like… I know those things are there, and I know folks might be expecting me to come by, but I just sit at home with the TV low. I don’t get much pleasure out of it, not like before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad most of the time. It’s more… flat, I guess. Like the house is quiet, and I sit there, and then I notice the dust on the TV stand—my sister used to say you could write your name in it if you didn’t keep up with it.\n\nBut sad… I’m not sure. I don’t sit and cry much or anything. It’s just hard to say what it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I don’t really weigh myself much. The scale’s in the bathroom but it’s tucked behind some towels, and I keep meaning to move it because it gets dusty there.\n\nMy clothes seem about the same, I guess. I haven’t noticed anything big like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I mean, sometimes I think about things I should’ve done, like calling somebody back, or keeping the kitchen nicer. My mother used to say dishes don’t wash themselves, and she was right about that.\n\nBut guilty all the time? I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have no interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s part of it. I used to like choir, and quilting too—my hands knew what to do with the squares, you know, lining them up just right. And visiting folks after church or when somebody expected me.\n\nNow I just don’t have the push for it. I’ll think, “I ought to go,” and then I’m still sitting there at home with the TV on low, not really watching it. People are probably waiting on me, but I just… don’t get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been… well, since I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. Time gets funny when you’re not getting up to go in every morning—Mondays feel like Tuesdays, and then the mail comes late and I think the whole day’s off.\n\nBut yes, it’s been building since then, little by little.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I get low and I sit too much, and sometimes I wonder what the point of a day is when nothing much happens, but ending my life… no, I haven’t really thought about doing that.\n\nI did have an aunt who used to say you don’t know what burdens people carry, and that always stuck with me. She kept all her old buttons in a coffee tin—funny what you remember. But no, doctor, I don’t believe I’ve had thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your lack of interest in activities and flat mood are interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does. I don’t keep up with things like I used to. I had to step back from leading choir, and I used to help with those little projects at the senior center—you know, cutting paper, sorting donations, whatever they needed. But then I’d say I would do something and it just wouldn’t get done.\n\nAnd people notice. The church ladies check on me more now, calling or stopping by, like they’re making sure I haven’t forgotten the world. It’s kind of them, but it makes me feel… well, like I’m not managing the way I ought to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your current state is worse than it was at the beginning?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is worse than when it started. At first, after I retired from the school office—oh, a little over two years ago now—it felt more like just adjusting, you know? Losing the routine, not hearing the copier jam or the children in the hall, all that noise you don’t think you’ll miss.\n\nBut it’s been building since then. Not all at once. More like when dust gathers on a shelf and you don’t notice until the sun hits it. So yes, I’d say it’s worse now than at the beginning.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless about your future?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know if I’d use that word. Some mornings I don’t think much past breakfast, if I’m honest. I’ll put the kettle on and then remember my neighbor used to say tea tastes different depending on the cup, which sounds silly, but she had these blue cups with little chips in them.\n\nBut about the future… I’m not sure. I don’t sit there thinking it’s all ruined or anything. It just feels kind of blank sometimes, like I can’t quite picture it. Is that what you mean by hopeless?\n\nDoctor: How many months would you say you've been feeling this flat and unmotivated?\n\nPatient: I guess… a little over two years, if I’m counting right. It started building after I retired from the school office. At first I thought it was just because I didn’t have my desk anymore, or the morning bell, and you know I kept all the paper clips in a little tin—funny the things you remember.\n\nBut yes… months-wise, more than twenty-four months. My mother used to say dishes don’t wash themselves, and she was right about that.\n\nBut guilty all the time? I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t really know.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have no interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s part of it. I used to like choir, and quilting too—my hands knew what to do with the squares, you know, lining them up just right. And visiting folks after church or when somebody expected me.\n\nNow I just don’t have the push for it. I’ll think, “I ought to go,” and then I’m still sitting there at home with the TV on low, not really watching it. People are probably waiting on me, but I just… don’t get myself moving.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been… well, since I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. Time gets funny when you’re not getting up to go in every morning—Mondays feel like Tuesdays, and then the mail comes late and I think the whole day’s off.\n\nBut yes, it’s been building since then, little by little.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about ending your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I get low and I sit too much, and sometimes I wonder what the point of a day is when nothing much happens, but ending my life… no, I haven’t really thought about doing that.\n\nI did have an aunt who used to say you don’t know what burdens people carry, and that always stuck with me. She kept all her old buttons in a coffee tin—funny what you remember. But no, doctor, I don’t believe I’ve had thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your lack of interest in activities and flat mood are interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does. I don’t keep up with things like I used to. I had to step back from leading choir, and I used to help with those little projects at the senior center—you know, cutting paper, sorting donations, whatever they needed. But then I’d say I would do something and it just wouldn’t get done.\n\nAnd people notice. The church ladies check on me more now, calling or stopping by, like they’re making sure I haven’t forgotten the world. It’s kind of them, but it makes me feel… well, like I’m not managing the way I ought to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your current state is worse than it was at the beginning?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is worse than when it started. At first, after I retired from the school office—oh, a little over two years ago now—it felt more like just adjusting, you know? Losing the routine, not hearing the copier jam or the children in the hall, all that noise you don’t think you’ll miss.\n\nBut it’s been building since then. Not all at once. More like when dust gathers on a shelf and you don’t notice until the sun hits it. So yes, I’d say it’s worse now than at the beginning.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless about your future?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know if I’d use that word. Some mornings I don’t think much past breakfast, if I’m honest. I’ll put the kettle on and then remember my neighbor used to say tea tastes different depending on the cup, which sounds silly, but she had these blue cups with little chips in them.\n\nBut about the future… I’m not sure. I don’t sit there thinking it’s all ruined or anything. It just feels kind of blank sometimes, like I can’t quite picture it. Is that what you mean by hopeless?\n\nDoctor: How many months would you say you've been feeling this flat and unmotivated?\n\nPatient: I guess… a little over two years, if I’m counting right. It started building after I retired from the school office. At first I thought it was just because I didn’t have my desk anymore, or the morning bell, and you know I kept all the paper clips in a little tin—funny the things you remember.\n\nBut yes… months-wise, more than twenty-four months. A little over two years.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your current state is significantly impacting your ability to take care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, in some ways. I mean, I still try to manage, but I’ve had to step back from things I used to do. I was leading choir for a while—well, helping lead, really, because Mrs. Donnelly always knew the alto parts better than me—and I used to help with projects at the senior center too.\n\nBut I don’t keep up with what I say I’ll do. I promise I’ll bring papers or call someone or organize something, and then it just… doesn’t happen right. So church friends check on me more now than they used to. They’ll call or stop by, which is kind of them, but it makes me feel a little embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your appetite?\n\nPatient: My appetite… I’m not sure it’s changed in any big way. I eat when I remember to, mostly. Breakfast is usually toast, unless the bread’s gone stale, and then I think about going to the shop but the shop moved the biscuits to a different aisle last year and I still don’t like that.\n\nBut, no… I haven’t really noticed a significant change, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your current state is severely impacting your daily life, making it difficult to carry out even the simplest tasks?\n\nPatient: Severely… I don’t know if I’d say severely, like I can’t do anything at all. I still get through some things.\n\nBut it does get in the way. I had to step back from helping lead choir, and from the senior-center projects too, because I’d say I’d do something—bring papers, call someone, sort things out—and then I just wouldn’t keep up with it. And you know, choir needs you to be there, especially when the altos get lost—though Mrs. Donnelly always had a good ear for that.\n\nSo… yes, it affects my daily life, but maybe not the very simplest things every single day. My church friends check on me more now than they used to. That part makes me feel a bit ashamed.\n\nDoctor: Has your current state lasted for more than two years?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s been building since I retired from the school office, and that was a little over two years ago now. I remember because they had that cake with the blue icing, and it got all over the napkins—funny the things you remember.\n\nBut yes… more than two years, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your lack of interest and flat mood are significantly interfering with your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does. I don’t mean to push people away, but I just don’t have the push I used to. Choir, quilting, going to visit folks… I used to do those things without thinking much about it.\n\nNow most days I’m just at home with the TV on low, and I know people are expecting me, or wondering where I am. The quilting group had those little squares they wanted finished—green ones, I think, though green never matches the way people think it will—and I just didn’t go.\n\nSo, yes… with friends, especially, it gets in the way. I don’t feel very much like joining in.\n\nDoctor: Has your current state of feeling flat and unmotivated lasted for less than two years or exactly two years or more?\n\nPatient: More than two years, yes… a little over, I think. It started building after I retired from the school office.\n\nThat was—well, I remember the office more than the date sometimes, the copier always jamming near the end of the day, and people leaving mugs everywhere. But yes, since I left there, it’s been going on. So not less than two years. A little over two.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
